Abstract: Interactions happen universally and spontaneously in daily lives. Studying involvement in interactions helps us analyze who can influence who and has a wide potential in applications. Specifically, we consider conversational involvement in free-standing conversations. We study two operationalizations of an FCG: F-formations and conversation floors, and propose that current assumption that F-formations capture single conversations is insufficient. To illustrate how different implementations could impact the detected F-formations, we employ three analyses. First, we propose that speaking status can be used for estimating conversation floors, and leveraged it to analyze floors within F-formations. Second, we analyze spatial characteristics of the detection results by applying different feature sets on chosen baselines, and find that groups resulting from head features are more likely to be subsumed by groups detected using other features. Finally, we do a joint analysis which combines the spatial features and floor patterns in the conversation. We observe that certain pipelines can detect groups with single floors from upper body features, while retaining F-formations with lower body features. We conclude that a more detailed theory and framework is needed to holistically demonstrate conversational involvement within interactions.

Abstract: Recent advances in zero-shot voice cloning systems make it possible to reproduce a recognizable voice from a short recording and attach it to arbitrary virtual characters. Prior work suggests that familiar vocal identity can carry over associated personality impressions to new targets such as robots or virtual characters. However, these works have mainly examined familiarity and personalization effects rather than directly testing whether a cloned familiar voice systematically transfers personality impressions to an embodied virtual character. Such transference raises the concern that cloned voices could be used to manipulate people into engaging with virtual characters as capable social actors. Across two studies, we investigated whether personality impressions transfer when a virtual character speaks with the cloned voice of a familiar person. Study 1 examined this question in a real-world university setting using the cloned voice of a lecturer familiar to the participating students. Study 2 examined the same question online using voices of social media content creators and members of their communities. Drawing on the concepts of identity activation, trait transfer, and person reconstruction, we show that evidence of personality transfer was limited in both studies. Observed effects were selective and did not generalize across traits or study contexts. In the online study in particular, recognizable voice identity cues activated expectations about the speaker but did not reconstruct the broader person impression. Qualitative responses further indicated that judgments were frequently shaped by perceived artificiality and by whether voice and appearance fit together. Our findings moderate common concerns that cloned voices straightforwardly manipulate people into perceiving artificial characters as capable social actors.

</details>

Abstract: Biomedical language models (LMs) provide strong representations for clinical tasks, but they often fail to capture the hierarchical structure among medical concepts. This limitation is especially pronounced for rare and fine-grained clinical codes in long-tailed vocabularies, where hierarchy-agnostic LM representations become less informative as concept frequency decreases. We introduce Semantic Medical Identifier (SMI), a training-free framework for constructing ontology-grounded Semantic IDs and hierarchy-aware medical concept representations. For each diagnosis, procedure, or medication concept, SMI defines its Semantic ID as the coarse-to-fine path through an expert-defined medical ontology. SMI then aggregates frozen biomedical LM embeddings across the corresponding ontology levels to form hierarchy-aware medical concept representations that preserve coarse-to-fine clinical semantics. The base SMI uses uniform aggregation across ontology levels, while a level-weighted variant selects global level weights on validation data. Our theoretical analysis shows that SMI yields more informative medical concept representations than hierarchy-agnostic LM embeddings, particularly for rare and fine-grained clinical concepts. Experiments show that SMI captures meaningful hierarchical structure and improves clinical relation prediction. On MIMIC-IV, SMI improves mortality prediction, readmission prediction, drug recommendation, and rare drug prediction. On eICU, SMI yields more robust patient representations under cross-hospital distribution shift.

Abstract: Large language models rely on prompt context to perform in-context learning, follow formats, and adapt to local evidence. This ability, however, also raises a robustness concern: models may reuse irrelevant tokens from earlier context when answering a later query. Prior work identifies this behavior as contextual entrainment and finds attention heads whose ablation reduces it, arguing that these heads are largely task- or relation-specific. But are the mechanisms behind contextual entrainment truly isolated by relation, or do relation-conditioned searches recover reusable components shared across relations? Using factual-distractor relation-completion prompts across 19 lexical-semantic subtypes, we identify attention heads whose ablation reduces entrainment and test how the resulting head sets transfer recovery ability across exact relations, semantic families, and mixed relation distributions. Same-family transfer exceeds different-family transfer, family residuals weaken once globally shared heads are removed, and subtype-specific searches repeatedly rediscover many global heads. Clean-completion and context-use probes further suggest that this shared intervention can reduce harmful copying while preserving ordinary relation completion and simple in-context abilities. Our findings provide a more precise perspective on contextual entrainment and its mitigation: harmful context following is not best addressed by searching for isolated relation-specific circuits, but by using heterogeneous attack prompts to reveal reusable intervention targets whose effects are shaped by semantic relation structure.

</details>

Abstract: Code-switching (CS), the alternation between languages within an utterance, is common in multilingual speech but underrepresented in pretraining corpora. Increasing CS coverage requires finding relevant utterances in large speech collections so that they can be included in training data. One way to find such utterances is to transcribe the speech and detect language alternation in the text, but this depends on obtaining accurate multilingual transcriptions. We propose a lightweight method that detects CS directly from speech. It uses frame-level language information in pretrained speech encoders to determine whether both languages are present within an utterance. Specifically, we identify language-selective hidden-state dimensions using monolingual recordings and aggregate their frame-level activations into a CS prediction. With a small CS set used to tune a decision rule, we show that the Whisper-small encoder achieves a mean F1 of $0.798$ across 14 language pairs in CS-FLEURS, compared with $0.776$ for a CNN and $0.751$ for a HuBERT linear baseline trained for monolingual language identification. Our findings suggest that pretrained speech encoders already encode the information needed to identify CS utterances, and they provide a lightweight tool for building more representative multilingual corpora.

</details>

Abstract: Rubric-based reward models (RMs) evaluate responses against explicit criteria. However, existing methods often derive criteria from observed response candidates, introducing response-induced bias that may limit rubric generalizability. Their judges also struggle with mechanical constraints when relying on reading alone, and commonly used pairwise judges provide only relative preferences, complicating their direct use for policy optimization. We propose ReVeR, a pointwise rubric RM that derives hard rules and graded principles by reasoning about the instruction alone and evaluates responses independently through tool-augmented judging. Both components are trained through label-blind distillation, in which preference labels only filter a teacher's outputs, yielding ReVeR-Traces, a large-scale dataset of rubric-reasoning and judging trajectories. Experiments demonstrate that ReVeR outperforms pairwise rubric RMs in average preference accuracy, most clearly on instruction following, as well as in test-time scaling and downstream policy optimization. We further find that generator RL improves preference accuracy while reducing rubric fidelity, and that higher preference accuracy does not necessarily translate into better downstream policies. These findings highlight that rubric fidelity, preference accuracy, and downstream utility are distinct properties, shedding light on future RM research.

</details>

Abstract: Pre-trained Large Language Model (LLMs) are effective on many tasks in zero-shot and few-shot settings. For machine translation of low-resource languages, the obvious options are few-shot prompting and fine-tuning on a small parallel set. We ask: what if moderate amounts of low-resource monolingual data are also available? We argue that, rather than continued pretraining, a more effective use of this data is the pre-LLM era technique of back-translation (BT). The current fine-tuned LLM translates monolingual sentences in each language, and the resulting pairs train it in the reverse direction. Updates on the parallel set are interleaved for stable learning. With 1K parallel pairs on Odia and Swahili, BT beats continued pretraining into the low-resource language by 7.7 to 16.4 XCOMET-XXL points in domain. With beam-decoded intermediate translations, it also beats a model with 10 time more parallel data. In-context examples retrieved from the parallel data further improve BT. We also analyze the source, quality and amount of monolingual text. Across five low-resource languages, with Hindi as a high-resource control, and two 3B backbones, BT improves translation into the low-resource language by 16 to 173% XCOMET-XXL over parallel-only fine-tuning. Blind human evaluation on Odia and Sanskrit agrees with the automatic metrics.

Abstract: How can we build an agent that thinks beyond human language, yet still acts through it in the external environment? Modern LLM agents still reason and interact through text, while latent reasoning offers an efficient internal medium but lacks native tool access and reasoning transparency. We bridge these two directions with Hybrid Reasoning Agents (HybridAgents), which dynamically navigate between latent reasoning and text-based interaction throughout a unified generation process. Architecturally, a lightweight HybridLink enables seamless transitions between text and latent modes, while the agent's continuous working memory preserves lossless hybrid context across multiple turns. These designs preserve tool-integrated agentic capabilities while making latent thoughts directly inspectable. Building on this architecture, we further develop dedicated post-training algorithms for hybrid reasoning: HybridSFT stabilizes tool-integrated hybrid generation by teaching the backbone to coordinate with HybridLink, then HybridRL scales reasoning by learning when to think in latent space and when to act through text. We evaluate HybridAgent at 3 model scales across 11 benchmarks covering mathematics, science, medicine, search, and agentic reasoning. Compared with frontier LLM Agents and agentic frameworks spanning both text-based and latent methods, HybridAgent consistently improves performance by 10.4%, while delivering 35.5%-54.7% token reduction, 1.8x-2.6x faster inference, and 2.7x-3.7x cost savings. Abstract: Reasoning models achieve strong performance through long chains of thought (CoT), but often spend more tokens than needed to solve a problem. Existing ways to shorten the CoT require additional training for each model, add a fixed steering vector to the model's hidden states, or use decoding heuristics such as stopping early or banning words like "wait", which can reduce accuracy. We propose DoubtLess, a training-free decoding method in which a much smaller reasoning model steers the large one towards brevity. The small model reads the large model's CoT so far twice, once prompted to be brief (the brief thinker) and once prompted to be verbose (the verbose thinker), and the difference between its two logit vectors, scaled by a strength, is added to the large model's logits at every step of the CoT. On seven benchmarks of competition mathematics, graduate-level science and competitive programming, DoubtLess shortens the CoT of Qwen3-32B by 31% with unchanged accuracy, outperforming five inference-time baselines. We also show that DoubtLess reduces thinking tokens per turn by 20% in a self-debugging coding agent with unchanged task performance. It also generalizes to two other model families with unchanged accuracy. When combined with training-based compression, it further shortens the CoT of a model already trained for brevity. Ablations and behavioral analysis show that DoubtLess reduces repeated checking, with most token savings coming after the correct answer first appears in the CoT. Our code is available at https://github.com/leoluolol/DoubtLess.

</details>

Abstract: Long-horizon agents have drawn growing attention for their ability to work autonomously -- from hours to days -- on economically valuable tasks, a capability essential to automated discovery and recursive self-improvement (RSI). Yet open-source models still struggle on long-horizon tasks, largely for lack of both training data and a working recipe, and scaling both is hard: long, complex tasks are difficult to curate and verify, and their rewards are sparse. We address both. First, we introduce Tev-Gym, the first long-horizon agent training dataset with verifiable, dense, progressive rewards -- 1,170 verified task environments across 7 domains. It is built on a latent-world-first approach that first constructs a latent world: a complete and correct hidden specification of the task domain, workflow, and expected state after each step. Each task is then progressively rendered from this specification into an agent-facing environment, enabling controllable complexity and a natural progress metric. Second, we turn this metric into a training recipe: via progress-potential reward shaping (PPRS), we convert progress into a dense per-step reward that improves training efficiency and makes otherwise-intractable tasks trainable. Training with this recipe yields Tev-27B, which achieves substantial gains on the Agents' Last Exam computing and math sciences domains (ALE-CM), matching GPT-6 Astra and Claude Opus 5 at Pass@5. Despite training only on ALE-related data, it generalizes to the Terminal-Bench (TB) 3.0 and 4.0 subsets, surpassing GLM 5.2 and Gemini 3.7 Flash, and reaches Claude Opus 4.8-level performance on middle-horizon tasks such as TB 2.1.

Abstract: Late-interaction (multi-vector) models represent documents using a set of token-level vectors, providing a middle ground between cross-encoders and dense encoders in terms of quality and cost of embedding. However, compared to the latter, the indices are costly to store and score. Pooling and pruning based compression techniques are deployed widely but deteriorate in quality noticeably with aggressive compression. We investigate whether a lightweight change to the scoring function can recover retrieval quality after document-vector compression. For each combination of encoder, compressor, and vector budget, we learn a global linear correction by distilling the uncompressed model's score distributions over candidate documents. The encoder and compressor remain frozen, and the correction can be applied to each query at search time or once, offline, to the stored vectors.
  We evaluate the method with ColBERTv2 and Jina v4 on MS MARCO and BEIR, and with ColPali2 on the ViDoRe v3 visual document retrieval benchmark. Experiments cover four training-free compression methods, multiple vector budgets, in-domain adaptation, and transfer from MS MARCO to BEIR. In the in-domain setting, the correction improves over baseline compression in 154 of 160 BEIR settings, while transfer from MS MARCO is more variable across models and datasets. We also find that matrices trained with synthetic queries can improve over baseline compression on ViDoRe v3. These results show that a small learned correction can recover some of the quality lost to compression without retraining the encoder or recompressing the documents.

Abstract: Training large-scale recommender systems on dynamic industrial platforms, such as Short-Form Video (SFV) services with billions of daily interactions, faces critical stability challenges. Rapid distribution shifts in user data and increasing model complexity frequently trigger a divergence cascade, typically initiating with gradient explosion, propagating to weight explosion, and culminating in loss explosion. This G-W-L cascade severely impedes model convergence and performance. This paper provides a systematic analysis of this G-W-L cascade within an industrial SFV recommender context. We then propose and evaluate a structured mitigation framework where interventions are explicitly mapped to counteract specific stages of the cascade: gradient (G) control, weight/activation (W) stabilization, and loss (L) shaping. Our contribution provides a structured framework for understanding and tackling training divergence, offering practical insights for deploying robust, continuously updated recommenders in large-scale, non-stationary settings. We empirically validate the effectiveness of these strategies on a major industrial SFV recommendation system.

</details>

Abstract: Structured knowledge retrieval acquires task-relevant evidence from structured knowledge sources for downstream tasks. Existing methods rely on unconstrained retrieval or predefined query-rule supervision, such as author-paper rules for scientific discovery and anatomy-drug rules for precision medicine. Such supervision is rarely available in practice. We propose Co-Adaptive Rule Routing and Constraining Retrieval (CoRE), which jointly learns which reasoning rule to assign to each query and how to retrieve evidence under that rule, without predefined query-rule pairs. A routing agent selects a rule, and its corresponding retriever traverses the knowledge base and ranks evidence that satisfies the rule's constraints. Because routing and retrieval are interdependent, CoRE alternates between reinforcement learning for the routing policy and contrastive learning for the rule-constrained retriever. The selected rules provide supervision for retriever training, while retrieval performance rewards the routing policy. This closed-loop process enables both components to co-adapt without explicit query-rule supervision. Experiments on STaRK-QA datasets across scientific discovery, biomedical reasoning, and e-commerce knowledge management demonstrate CoRE's effectiveness. Ablations show the benefit of co-adapting the router and retriever. Generation-based routing transfers across datasets and benefits from multi-domain pretraining followed by target-specific fine-tuning. Analysis of rule usage across queries also informs automatic rule refinement and discovery.

</details>

Abstract: In the era of big data, precise event retrieval from large video collections has become increasingly important and challenging. In particular, locating multiple events from the same video in their temporal order is difficult when different events are described by different types of evidence, such as visual content, on-screen text, and spoken words. We develop EVISTA, a unified multimodal video retrieval system for the Ho Chi Minh City AI Challenge 2026, which integrates visual-semantic, OCR, ASR, object, and metadata search over a shared keyframe index, and propose TEMPO-P, an evidence-aware temporal path optimization method for ordered multi-event retrieval. Building EVISTA for this task reveals two limitations of independent event retrieval: a relevant video may be missed when one event is weakly retrieved, and independently retrieved event candidates may be temporally inconsistent or unable to represent events defined primarily by text or speech. TEMPO-P addresses these limitations by aggregating candidate videos across events and modalities, jointly scoring keyframes using visual, OCR, and ASR evidence, and selecting a compact temporally ordered path within each video through dynamic programming. On five organizer queries and 36 additional multi-event queries, TEMPO-P matches visual-only temporal alignment on visually described events (R@1 0.42 for both) and retrieves queries containing text-defined events, which visual-only alignment cannot represent (R@1 0.38). TEMPO-P is integrated into EVISTA as its ordered-event retrieval mode.

</details>

Abstract: Dynamic Product Advertising (DPA) retrieval systems are a core component of modern e-commerce advertising; however, their evaluation remains challenging. Conventional offline engagement metrics are limited by sparse, biased feedback, while online evaluation is costly and time-consuming. In this work, we propose a user--product relevance metric for offline evaluation of DPA retrieval systems. The metric first compresses the engagement history into a compact set of intent representatives via Semantic-ID-grouped, event-stratified segmentation, then assesses the relevance of a recommended product to each individual intent and aggregates them as a user-level relevance score, yielding a stable and interpretable measure of retrieval quality. To compute this metric at scale, we leverage large language models (LLMs) as semantic relevance judges, capturing nuanced functional and semantic relationships that heuristic methods often miss. We propose an agentic, human-aligned prompting framework that generates multi-category relevance guidelines and distills teacher-model judgments into an efficient student model for scalable evaluation. The resulting metric strongly correlates with offline and online business metrics, enabling faster and more reliable DPA retrieval iteration before A/B testing.

</details>

Abstract: Embedding models underpin modern job-candidate matching: resumes, job descriptions, and queries are encoded as dense vectors, and recommendations come from nearest-neighbor retrieval over that space. Any social bias in the embeddings therefore feeds directly into HR recommendations. We audit six multilingual sentence-embedding models (LaBSE- and E5-family, both public and custom fine-tuned) using Bias-in-Bios (occupational gender bias) and StereoSet (stereotypes across profession, race, gender, and religion). We treat statistical validity as a first-class concern: we identify a non-independence error in a naive significance test, where pooling non-independent pairwise similarities inflated a trivial 0.0046 mean difference to $p \approx 0$, and replace it with a profession-level paired Wilcoxon signed-rank test, individual-resampling bootstrap confidence intervals, and permutation tests for StereoSet. Under the corrected methodology, knowledge-distillation (KD) fine-tuning of E5-Base significantly increases measured bias on Bias-in-Bios ($Δ= -0.177$, $p < 10^{-8}$; more negative means more bias), although this measure is sensitive to global embedding geometry. The KD result is corroborated by pro-stereotype patterns in the StereoSet profession category (uncorrected $p < 0.05$). Binary-classification fine-tuning of the same base model shows no statistically distinguishable change ($p = 0.218$). Fine-tuning strategy is thus an actionable design lever for practitioners building HR recommenders. We also report which benchmark categories are underpowered, and note that two domain-adapted models could not be included in significance testing due to a software-access constraint, which limits our conclusions.

</details>

Abstract: Adapting embedding models to new tasks requires training data that reflect task-specific information needs and relevance criteria. However, manual data design is costly and difficult to scale. Here we introduce SYNTRA, a novel retrieval data synthesis method whose self-improvement loop iteratively refines reusable synthesis instructions using feedback on trial outputs. This process requires only an unlabelled corpus and a few relevant query-document pairs. Experiments across 24 retrieval datasets spanning 15 domains demonstrate the effectiveness of the synthesized data. These data also improve widely used, strong embedding models on tasks with and without existing labelled training sets. Self-improvement enhances retrieval on all six evaluated tasks, outperforming human calibration on four. Moreover, retrieval performance continues to improve on MS MARCO as we scale the training dataset to 32 times its initial size. Overall, with minimal human intervention, SYNTRA enables scalable adaptation of embedding models across domains and tasks.

</details>

Abstract: This report studies passage retrieval and re-ranking on the MS MARCO benchmark using lightweight Qwen3-0.6B models. Across three experimental settings --- BM25-then-reranking task, corpus-wide dense retrieval task, and dense retrieval then re-ranking task --- the results demonstrate that competitive ranking performance can be achieved without complex architectural modifications, custom loss functions, or cross-encoder distillation. Instead, substantial gains stem from data-centric strategies: corpus-targeted continued pre-training, rigorous data curation, hard-negative mining, training--inference distribution alignment, and weight merging via Model Soup. In particular, Model Soup delivers marked performance gains while preserving the inference efficiency of a single model. These findings highlight how data curation and weight merging can push sub-billion-parameter models to state-of-the-art performance.

Abstract: Generative search engines such as ChatGPT, Gemini and Perplexity now decide which Web pages users see as sources, and a growing industry of generative engine optimization advises publishers on how to earn these citations. We study what shapes citation in two 90-day collections of answers to commercial questions in contrasting markets: 158,099 answers with 468,037 citations. Comparing pages of the same website, content matters in one way: pages whose title and paragraphs match the questions people ask are cited more, whereas FAQ blocks, structured data, summary boxes and page speed appear effective only across websites. Page properties nevertheless explain little of the variation between pages of a website. Replaying the questions through the engines' APIs shows why. Every page ChatGPT cites is one its own search retrieved, and which pages that search retrieves, for sub-queries the model rewrites on every answer and that rarely coincide with Google's or Bing's results, explains far more of the variation than page properties (32% against 2.8%). Content acts mainly through this step, by matching the engine's queries in meaning. The model's own knowledge shapes it too: ChatGPT writes brands it knows into its searches, prefers websites it knows, and names brands without any supporting page, and controlled experiments show that brand names reorder its rankings of products with identical facts. Pages are thus cited when searched; brands are also named when remembered. The findings replicate in a market of small B2B vendors. We release data and code.

</details>

Abstract: Conversational query rewriting makes context-dependent requests explicit for retrieval, but additional context can introduce an information need the user did not express. This raises a question about the interaction between query fidelity and retrieval breadth. This paper examines that interaction in behavior-adaptive conversational search, using iKAT SCAI 2026 as the evaluation setting. We compare five query-generation configurations within a behavior-adaptive assistant using BM25 and SPLADE retrieval. A minimal-faithful resolver preserves the user's wording and resolves only context-dependent references; weighted reciprocal rank fusion combines its rankings with those from a learned rewriter and the raw utterance. Offline evaluation shows that adding these formulations recovers recall over the faithful query alone, with a slight reduction in nDCG@10. Across our five iKAT SCAI 2026 submissions, the weighted configuration obtains the highest mean retrieval scores, reaching nDCG@5 of 0.534, a 5.3\% relative improvement over the strongest alternative. However, this retrieval advantage does not coincide with the highest conversational satisfaction scores. These findings motivate separating faithful query interpretation from retrieval expansion, while assessing their effects on conversational utility independently.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-hop retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) requires finding documents whose evidence is useful in combination. Query relevance and graph connectivity help locate related content, but neither directly measures the additional information one document contributes given another. We introduce SG$^2$RAG, a framework that represents this complementarity through semantic gain. Offline, a large language model assesses the relevant and nonredundant information contributed by one chunk given another within neighborhoods proposed by semantic similarity. These ordered assessments are aggregated into symmetric edge weights to construct a reusable Semantic Gain Graph. Online, controlled Personalized PageRank starts from query-relevant seeds and ranks complementary evidence beyond the initial retrieval set. Experiments on MuSiQue, 2Wiki, and HotpotQA with two language model backbones show that SG$^2$RAG achieves the best average EM, F1, and Recall@10 among the evaluated single-retrieval methods. Under identical candidate support and propagation, semantic gain improves average F1 and Recall@10 over similarity weighting by 4.08 and 4.31 points. Analyses of supporting documents and improvements in three iterative retrieval frameworks further support its effectiveness as a reusable retrieval component.

</details>

Abstract: Generative engines such as ChatGPT Web retrieve webpages to answer user queries, but only a subset are shown as citations. Understanding this retrieval-to-citation process is important for generative engine optimization (GEO), which aims to improve content visibility in generative-engine responses. However, prior studies mainly analyze final responses, leaving the internal execution process and its variation across model settings less understood. To this end, we conduct an observational study of four ChatGPT Web settings from ChatGPT 5.2 to 5.5, covering Instant and Thinking modes at the URL, hostname, and entity levels. We examine how ChatGPT rewrites queries, retrieves candidate webpages, and selects references. The Instant settings remain mostly single-round, while GPT-5.4 Thinking frequently performs multiple retrieval rounds. Across repeated runs, candidates retrieved more often are also more likely to be cited. We then evaluate Google Search and the OpenAI Responses API as external proxies. Responses API candidates provide broader coverage, while its citations more closely match sources cited by ChatGPT Web. Both proxies match ChatGPT Web better at the hostname and entity levels than at the exact-URL level. Even when combined, they recover only about one quarter of cited URLs. We further test whether these external signals remain predictive when combined. Within the external candidate set, a supervised model combining them reaches AUCs above 0.74 for GPT-5.3 and GPT-5.5 at all three levels. These findings suggest that GEO should distinguish retrieval visibility from citation visibility, account for rewritten queries and run-to-run variation, and consider hostname- and entity-level presence in addition to exact URLs. Differences across settings also suggest that GEO strategies should be re-evaluated when the deployed setting or serving environment changes.

</details>

Abstract: Multimodal recommender systems effectively alleviate data sparsity and cold-start problems by integrating heterogeneous information, yet complex cross-modal representations and model decision-making processes also introduce new interpretability challenges. Despite the rapid growth of research on the interpretability of multimodal recommendation in recent years, relevant methods and evaluation criteria remain relatively fragmented, lacking a unified conceptual framework. To address this gap, this paper categorizes existing methods into modality explanation and post-hoc explanation based on the timing and manner of explanation generation, and further constructs a progressive system for each category. Building on this foundation, this paper synthesizes four categories of evaluation metrics from both objective quantitative and subjective perceptual perspectives, and reviews relevant datasets along the dimensions of application domain and data source. Through a comprehensive analysis of existing research, we further propose future research directions. This paper aims to establish a unified analytical perspective across multimodal representation, recommendation decision-making, and user preferences, providing a research foundation for building transparent and trustworthy multimodal recommender systems.

</details>

Abstract: Federated Recommendation Systems (FedRSs) learn collaborative scores from interaction histories on device and rank items by them, so a higher score means a higher rank. Item text and images add semantic information that can reorder the list. Existing Federated Multimodal Recommenders (FedMRs) fuse this content into their models, which can obscure how content reorders the list. We therefore propose the SEmantic Residual Adapter (SERA), which keeps content out of an existing FedRS and adds semantic residual scores to its collaborative scores. Content can then lift an item above another only when its semantic residual score exceeds the other's by more than their collaborative score gap. This score grows with the item's content similarity to the user's interaction history and is capped at a residual bound. We prove that for any content, an item can enter the list only if its collaborative score is within twice this bound of the lowest one in the list. A larger bound lets content lift more items, including irrelevant ones, so the bound trades off how far content reaches against accuracy. With a validated bound, SERA improves all three FedRSs on all six datasets without adding personalized parameters. It outperforms FedMRs in most comparisons and lifts some FedRSs above centralized baselines.

</details>

Abstract: Collaborative filtering predicts user-item interactions from implicit feedback. We focus on matrix factorization (MF) trained with the Bayesian Personalized Ranking loss. This loss updates each user embedding only through that user's observed items and sampled negatives, so sparse feedback gives MF few constraints. To constrain each user through other users, we propose Pathwise Alignment for Collaborative Filtering (PACF), which trains MF with one network shared by all training pairs. At points between user and item embeddings, a flow matching term trains the network to predict the user-to-item step, reversed and scaled for sampled negatives. A trajectory consistency term pulls each user's predicted path to end at the observed item. Both terms update the embeddings, so the network is discarded after training, thereby maintaining the efficiency of MF at inference. We prove that a network with bounded sensitivity to its input point accurately fits users who share an item only if their embeddings are close, so these users constrain one another. Against eleven discriminative and generative baselines, PACF achieves the highest performance on all seven datasets. Its relative gains over MF are largest where feedback is sparsest. The shared network thus supplies the missing constraints through other users.

</details>

Abstract: Multimodal graph recommendation integrates observed interactions with item images and text to learn user preferences. These inputs constitute Semantic-Behavioral Evidence (SBE). Many existing graph methods incorporate collaborative and multimodal semantic relations, yet their aggregation and state updates remain context-separated, limiting the continuous composition of preference evidence across layers and the joint use of multiple sources. To address this limitation, we propose CC-MGRec, a context-coupled graph learning method that organizes SBE within a common graph learning context for cross-relation modeling and compositional propagation. Its context-coupled multimodal graph integrates user-item collaborative relations and item-item semantic relations, coordinating their message scales through joint normalization. Recommendation-grounded semantic calibration uses training behavior to adjust semantic neighbor weights while preserving normalized collaborative propagation coefficients. Collaborative-semantic compositional propagation updates shared node states layer by layer to continuously compose collaborative and multimodal semantic evidence, implicitly forming Preference Evidence Chains (PECs), including collaborative-semantic and visual-textual PECs. Joint execution of relation messages on the same shared node states within each layer preserves propagation outputs while reducing intermediate output processing and fusion overhead. We provide theoretical analysis of the proposed mechanisms. Experiments on three Amazon datasets demonstrate improved recommendation quality and faster inference during graph propagation.

</details>

Abstract: Resolving correct citations can be a time-consuming task for research writers. We address local citation recommendation (LCR), where the goal is to retrieve appropriate references for a given sentence. Web tools generally depend on the existing citation graph, which may bias results toward citation count or more recent publications. LCR research using full paper texts has been limited by the lack of publicly available data. We present a new dataset, the Local Citation Recommendation Corpus (LCRC), containing 14,735 scientific statements derived from astrophysics review papers, each with one or more citation targets whose full text is accessible via API. Using this dataset we systematically compare retrieval methods and develop a citation recommendation system that avoids citation graph bias while achieving 8.0% improvement in Recall@25 and 6.9% improvement in NDCG@25 over dense retrieval baselines without model fine-tuning. To support reproducibility, we make our code and dataset publicly available.

Abstract: Scientific claims shared through social media platforms frequently reference research findings without explicitly linking to the corresponding scientific publications. Automatically retrieving the correct scientific source for such claims is a challenging multilingual retrieval problem involving semantic matching, paraphrasing, noisy social media text, and cross-domain language variation. In this work, we present a multilingual two-stage retrieval framework for CLEF 2026 CheckThat! Task 1 on Source Retrieval for Scientific Web Claims. Our system combines fine-tuned dense retrieval models with cross-encoder reranking for improving multilingual scientific document retrieval. We evaluate multiple transformer-based dense retrieval architectures including multilingual-E5, BGE, and Contriever under different metadata configurations. We further investigate multilingual joint training, retrieval depth analysis, metadata enrichment, and language balancing strategies for improving retrieval effectiveness. Experimental results demonstrate that metadata enrichment consistently improves retrieval effectiveness, while fine-tuned cross-encoder reranking provides additional gains in retrieval precision. Our final multilingual system achieves MRR@5 scores of 0.6222 for English, 0.5112 for German, and 0.6254 for French, with an overall average MRR@5 score of 0.5862.

Abstract: Large-scale personalization is shifting from embedding-table retrieval to LLM-based recommenders that generate the Semantic ID (SID) of the item to recommend, where each SID is a short sequence of discrete hierarchical content codes. A single instruction-tuned model can then unify retrieval, content understanding, and language, and recommend brand-new titles from their content alone using SID; then, how should the model generate the SID? Traditionally, models rely on a single language modeling head that generates tokens autoregressively, typically using constrained beam search. In contrast, an emerging body of work explores multi-token prediction (MTP), where dedicated heads predict all tokens in parallel, substantially reducing the decoding latency associated with beam search. We report an industry-scale, apples-to-apples comparison of these two generation paradigms: single-head autoregressive decoding vs. MTP. We introduce a new auxiliary ranking loss, defined over the joint SID score, to train the MTP objective. We evaluate the SID training objectives on both quality and training/inference cost, inside a multi-task LLM that jointly does personalized retrieval, content question-answering (QA), and instruction following over a large streaming-media catalog. Our experiments show single-head beam search consistently beats MTP on most product-critical metrics: ranking and content QA, and trains at roughly 2.6x lower dollar cost on Qwen3-8B scale; MTP wins only on raw inference throughput. Around this finding, we detail an efficient training and inference stack and report end-to-end gains over the previous non-SID baseline equipped with an auxiliary ranking head: +77% item cold-start ranking, +22% content-QA accuracy, +2.8x faster training, with language metric regression traced to SID vocabulary expansion. We address the language regression by reinforcement learning post training.

</details>

Abstract: E-commerce user preferences evolve dynamically within sessions, exhibiting concurrent and multi-centric interest distributions. While existing multimodal sequential recommendation methods leverage global attention to compress historical interactions, they inadequately capture the concurrent nature of multi-interest behaviors and fail to model the underlying multi-centric distribution of user intent. To tackle this issue, we propose OT-FlowRec, an optimal transport-guided multi-bridge flow matching framework that explicitly models multi-interest distributions to facilitate multimodal sequential recommendation. Unlike single-path generative models, OT-FlowRec constructs a mixture of parallel, interest-specific transport flows to model the multi-centric distribution characteristics, where each flow preserves a distinct user interest cluster via an optimal transport plan. Then, a geometric transport mechanism is designed to establish minimum-cost paths from the latent multi-interest distribution to target preference prototypes to provide multi-interest awareness. Moreover, a progressive time-sampling warmup strategy dynamically biases optimization weights toward the final states of the generation process, bridging the gap between continuous global distribution fitting and practical top-$k$ ranking metrics. This approach achieves explicit interest disentanglement, ensuring the preservation of a multi-peaked preference representation instead of collapsing it into a unimodal average. Extensive experiments on three public datasets demonstrate that OT-FlowRec outperforms existing state-of-the-art baselines.

</details>

Abstract: Semantic-ID-based generative recommendation unifies retrieval and ranking through autoregressive decoding but differs structurally from discriminative models, which can directly access rich target-item side features. The attributes are compressed into a finite codebook during quantization, while the target item remains undetermined during decoding and its features cannot be looked up. This target-side feature vacuum weakens generation at each semantic-ID level through high conditional entropy and cascading errors, and final ranking through missing target-side feature crossing.
  Existing methods interact with candidate representations or generate discrete attribute tokens, but none reconstructs a continuous target-side representation inside decoding to support both stepwise generation and ranking. We propose TacGR (Stepwise Target-Side Feature Anticipation and Crossing for Generative Recommendation). Before decoding each codeword, TacGR anticipates a continuous target-side feature vector and crosses it with the semantic prefix and user behavior sequence inside the decoder. The crossed representation conditions current-level generation and is directly used for ranking.
  Continuous-vector regression risks a learnable target collapsing with its prediction and prediction noise propagating into generation through attention. TacGR addresses these challenges with a semantic-anchoring auxiliary tower and a unidirectional visibility mask, respectively. For extremely long industrial behavior histories, it introduces gated convolutional denoising to suppress noise and adapts the pyramid memory funnel to reduce inference cost. Offline experiments on a large-scale Lazada dataset show significant gains over OneRec, UniRec, and UniSGR. Online A/B testing improves IPV, Order, and GMV by 0.79%, 5.55%, and 3.09%, respectively.

</details>

Abstract: Memory ranking is the stage in personalized Large Language Models (LLMs) that decides which of the candidate memories returned by a retriever enter the limited context, so that the answer is grounded in the right parts of the user's history. Existing memory rankers operate in a single pass, scoring each candidate once against the raw query and cutting the ranked list at a fixed top-K. The single pass misses memories that the answer needs but that look unrelated to the query on their own, and the fixed cut ignores how many memories the query needs. To address both problems, we propose ReMind, a memory ranker that runs as a recurrent and self-terminating loop, following the cognitive theory of memory search: each recalled item joins the cue for the next recall (compound cueing), and recall stops once enough has been collected for the task (metamemory monitoring). For compound cueing, ReMind adds the top-ranked memory to the query context at each step and reranks the remaining candidates under the expanded cue, so that each recall makes the cue more specific and later steps look for what is still missing rather than for what merely resembles the query. For metamemory monitoring, a sufficiency predictor estimates at each step whether the collected memories are enough to answer the query and stops the loop once they are, so that the number of selected memories adapts to each query. Experiments on public benchmarks show that ReMind achieves the best results compared to state-of-the-art memory rankers.

</details>

Abstract: Harnessing rich modality features has recently gained prominence in multi-modal recommendation systems (MRSs). To delineate user modality preferences, existing works focus on modality fusion, which can be achieved via attentive summation or concatenation. In this work, we introduce the modality inhibitance effect - a phenomenon which limits existing MRSs when these models transit from dual-modal to tri-modal configurations resulting in significant performance degradation. In particular, apart from the modal-specific contamination encapsulated in the newly introduced modality, the inclusion of new features results in excessive informational redundancy, thereby severely impeding effective integration within current approaches. To address the modality inhibitance effect, we propose a new multi-scale modality wavelet (MMWave) multi-modal recommender, which aims to fully capitalize on essential modality features and suppress modal-specific noise. This is acheived by employing wavelet decomposition from a multi-scale (time \& frequency) perspective, ensuring that only essential (non-redundant) features of each modality are extracted. We then account for the latent associations between item modality features by designing a new structure-aware multi-modal graph learning module that captures high-order latent structural information pertaining to items. To modulate the relative contributions of modality features and achieve effective integration, we then propose a new adaptive modality regulator component that employs a new cross-weight integration strategy. Experiments conducted on four real-world datasets validate the efficacy of our proposed model. The source code for this work has been made publicly available at https://github.com/kennethorq/MMWave.

</details>

Abstract: Relevance prediction between user queries and items is a fundamental component of e-commerce search systems. Traditionally, high-quality relevance labels are obtained through human judgment, which is reliable but expensive, slow, and difficult to scale. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) enable automated relevance judgment at scale, achieving performance comparable to human annotators. However, such systems often rely on large commercial LLMs with complex prompts, resulting in high inference costs and limited throughput.
  In this work, we propose a framework that bridges this gap by distilling a high-performing LLM-based relevance judgment system into efficient multimodal student models. We first curate a small, high-quality human-annotated dataset to serve as ground truth. Using this dataset, we develop a reliable LLM-as-a-judge pipeline through careful prompt engineering and iterative refinement. This pipeline is then used to generate large-scale relevance labels, which serve as training data for compact multimodal models. Our distilled models achieve performance close to the teacher system while significantly improving latency, cost, and scalability. This approach enables practical deployment of high-quality relevance prediction systems in production environments.

Abstract: Generative retrieval (GR) offers a promising paradigm for industrial search by unifying candidate generation within an autoregressive framework. However, its effectiveness is limited by two key challenges. First, existing Semantic ID (SID) tokenizers struggle to incorporate collaborative signals while maintaining effective codebook utilization, limiting their ability to distinguish items and achieve high-quality retrieval. Second, existing reinforcement learning (RL) methods suffer from severe reward sparsity under exact SID matching, leaving many sampled trajectories without effective learning signals and hindering preference optimization. To address these challenges, we propose WeGR, a unified generative retrieval framework consisting of two complementary components. Specifically, the Collaborative and Collapse-free Tokenizer (CoCo-VAE) incorporates query-to-item and item-to-item collaborative signals while mitigating codebook collapse through codebook refresh and complementary regularization, enabling more informative and balanced SID representations. Building upon these SIDs, Dynamic Horizon Policy Optimization (DHPO) progressively expands both reward evaluation and policy optimization horizons, reducing learning difficulty while leveraging behavioral reward signals at different levels of the SID hierarchy, without relying on auxiliary reward models. Extensive offline experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of WeGR in improving retrieval and ranking performance. Moreover, a two-month online A/B test in WeChat E-commerce Search demonstrates relative improvements of 2.539% in orders, 1.508% in click-through rate, and 1.381% in conversion rate, while reducing online CPU and GPU usage by 25.5% and 26.1%, respectively. The system has been successfully deployed across multiple search scenarios in WeChat, serving billions of users, generating tens of millions of PVs daily.

</details>

Abstract: Generative recommendation models have shown promising results by representing items as semantic IDs and generating these IDs autoregressively. We introduce an approach showing that explicitly incorporating hierarchical and attribute-level supervision into generative recommendation can simultaneously improve training efficiency and long-tail recommendation performance. Existing approaches typically optimize semantic ID prediction with a standard causal cross-entropy objective, which does not explicitly exploit the hierarchical structure of semantic IDs and can require substantial training to learn effective item representations. Moreover, data-sparse tail items often suffer from poor representations due to limited supervision, resulting in weaker long-tail recommendation performance. We address these challenges with HASTE (Hierarchical and Attribute-aware Supervision for generaTive rEcommendation), a unified training framework that introduces hierarchical and attribute-specific supervision. HASTE's Hierarchical Loss explicitly exploits the multi-level structure of semantic IDs, providing supervision at each level of the item hierarchy and substantially accelerating training convergence. Its Attribute-Specific Losses encourage the model to preserve fine-grained item attributes, leveraging the language model's inherent semantic knowledge to improve representations and recommendations for tail items. We extensively evaluate HASTE on a fashion recommendation benchmark and across multiple domains of the Amazon dataset. HASTE improves recommendation performance across datasets and item-frequency regimes. Notably, the Hierarchical Loss achieves approximately 2x faster convergence than the standard causal cross-entropy objective, substantially improving training efficiency while maintaining strong recommendation quality.

</details>

Abstract: Generative retrieval is known for its efficient end-to-end indexing and retrieval. In particular, numeric Document Identifiers (DocIDs) are generally more efficient because they are shorter and generally fixed-length sequences over a small vocabulary, requiring fewer decoding steps during beam search. However, they tend to generalize poorly to unseen documents and must be learned during indexing-stage training. In this paper, we propose a numeric DocID design for generative retrieval that enables better adaptation to unseen documents without retraining.Our DocID design inherits the language model's knowledge: each DocID is a sequence of tokens factorized into bits, and each token is scored as the sum of its bits. Unlike existing numeric DocIDs, whose tokens carry meaning only through embeddings memorized during indexing training, each bit here derives its meaning from the model's representation. Therefore, the DocIDs of unseen documents remain scorable without retraining.Experiments on three benchmarks show that our DocID design substantially improves unseen-document retrieval while maintaining comparable performance on seen documents. It achieves over 20 percentage points higher Hit@10 on unseen documents and up to 7 times lower inference latency than existing numeric DocID designs on NQ320K and TriviaQA, while performing only 3 percentage points below weight-update methods on MS MARCO without additional training. These results show that an appropriate DocID design can heavily reduce retraining costs when adapting generative retrievers to new documents.

</details>

Abstract: Filtering search is one of the crucial applications of approximate nearest neighbor search (ANNS). In filtering search, label information is used in conjunction with unstructured vector data. ANNS provides efficient similarity search in large-scale datasets, however, the memory footprint of storing both vector data and their associated label becomes a significant cost factor as dataset sizes grow. To address this problem of increasing DRAM usage, we propose AiSAQ-in-Filtering, with a novel quantization method called LiPQ (Label-inclusive Product Quantization). LiPQ combines unstructured data and structured labels into a single representation, leading no growth of encoded data. Our proposal increased recall@1 of single-label searches by up to 55% and fewer disk I/Os compared with existing methods. This also retains the main advantages of AiSAQ, which can reduce DRAM footprint by up to 1/78 and index switching time comparing with existing methods. This proposal can also be applied to sparse-dense hybrid search beyond label filtering such as semantic and keyword search of documents.

Abstract: Modern conversational information access systems continuously extract user information from conversations to build user profiles for personalized retrieval. As user profiles accumulate over time, they inevitably become noisy, making effective user profile denoising a fundamental challenge. Existing personalized conversational query reformulation methods rely on the black-box reasoning capability of LLMs to expand the original query with relevant user profile phrases, producing a verbose query for retrieval. Such rewrite-then-retrieve pipelines reduce user profile denoising to the explicit selection of profile phrases in the sparse token space, separating personalization from downstream relevance modeling. Consequently, they often fail to identify user information that is truly beneficial for retrieval.
  To enable retrieval-aware user profile denoising, we propose Personalized Conversational Dense Retrieval (PCDR), which learns to jointly project the conversation history and user profile into a latent embedding space dedicated to retrieval. By inheriting the context denoising capability from conversational dense retrievers, PCDR requires only a handful of personalized relevance annotations for training. We evaluate PCDR on the iKAT 2026 tasks, where it serves as the core retrieval component of a conversational AI agent assisting users with complex information needs.

</details>

Abstract: A model trained on readers' highlights can show a newcomer where earlier readers converged on a page, even on pages with few readers. A year of stronger language models suggests two upgrades: use them directly, or use them as teachers. We compare both with a third, more reader data, on one social highlighting platform, with the comparisons, the selection rules and a sealed set of fresh pages fixed in advance. On 215 development pages, seven language models released by October 2026 rank sentences zero-shot at an average precision of at most 0.3744 against the sentences half the readers marked most, and 0.3931 when fused with reading position, while a 3-billion-parameter model trained on reader highlights alone reaches 0.4346. Used as the teacher that breaks ties among readers' counts, the stronger fusion changes a trained model by +0.0013 [-0.0012, +0.0040], whereas going from half to all of its training pages adds +0.0242 [+0.0179, +0.0301]. In this recipe, more reader data improves a trained model where a stronger tie-breaking teacher yields no material gain, and frontier models used as rankers still trail a small model trained on readers.

Abstract: Open-Web streams can reference real-world events without making them suitable for organization: a correctly linked event may serve only as background, while an event absent from the knowledge base may be central to an item. This distinction reveals an evaluation blind spot in multistage Web systems: pipelines determine not only how inputs are scored, but also which inputs receive scores at all. Using open-Web event admission as a case study, we examine three connected objects (the scored population, the selected operating point, and behavior on the complete input stream) through shared-subset comparisons, admission-loss decomposition, and controlled extraction-recovery interventions. We find that reported ranking differences depend on the scored population, scoring gaps exhibit distinct backbone-dependent failure modes, and recovering missing scores can increase false acceptance under an unchanged threshold. On Original-302, Qwen candidate validity scores 255 of 302 items while direct admission scores all 302; evaluating both on the shared subset narrows their AUC gap. For Mistral, span-safe recovery raises false acceptance from 73.1% to 92.5% with the threshold fixed. Across 602 human-annotated evaluation items within a 3,290-record resource, these findings establish a reporting framework that considers score availability, threshold selection, and complete-stream decisions together.

Abstract: Popularity bias is a well-known challenge in recommender systems, often leading to the over-exposure of popular items and reduced catalog coverage. In this work, we propose PR-entmax, a sparse prior-regularized objective that makes popularity correction context-dependent. We derive our method from a generalized variational principle in which an item popularity prior is incorporated directly into a sparse probability mapping, yielding a popularity-aware active support: the set of items receiving non-zero probabilities and hence non-zero gradients is determined jointly by contextual relevance and item popularity. As a result, the prior affects not only item probabilities, but also which items participate in gradient updates during training. The method requires no architectural changes and can be integrated into existing training pipelines by modifying only the loss function. At inference time, the prior term is removed, and items are ranked by the learned contextual relevance alone. Experiments across multiple benchmark datasets and sequential recommendation architectures show that PR-entmax consistently reduces popularity bias while preserving or improving recommendation relevance, advancing the relevance-debiasing trade-off.

</details>

Abstract: Evaluating fashion retrieval systems remains challenging due to the limited availability of benchmarks that capture the fine-grained, open-ended queries encountered in real-world search and recommendation. Existing benchmarks often rely on manual curation, predefined taxonomies, or synthetic prompts, limiting scalability and semantic coverage. We introduce FASHION-RQ: Mining Hierarchical Semantic Structure for Automated Evaluation of Fashion Retrieval, an automated framework that derives user-type queries and relevance sets directly from catalog structure. Our key insight is that hierarchical discrete representations learned by Residual Quantized Variational Autoencoders (RQ-VAE) organize products into semantically coherent clusters at progressively finer granularities. We leverage this structure to construct FASHION-RQBENCH, a scalable benchmark without manual query annotation or predefined taxonomies. We propose four complementary tasks for evaluating ranking, relevance classification, fine-grained sibling discrimination, and paraphrase robustness, alongside query- and attribute-level relevance metrics. Experiments across recent retrieval models demonstrate that FASHION-RQBENCH differentiates model capabilities across query types and attributes. A human study further shows that benchmark queries achieve comparable naturalness and higher intent clarity than real user queries, supporting their validity for real-world evaluation. The code and FASHION-RQBENCH dataset will be publicly released upon acceptance.

Abstract: Dense vector retrieval can miss evidence connected through intermediate entities, while direct graph retrieval requires access to the graph at query time. We compare five offline graph-based embedding transformations and strategies for using the resulting graph-modified embeddings alongside the originals, without query-time graph access. On a synthetic professional-network benchmark with complete supporting-evidence sets, direct replacement of the original embeddings reduces evidence recovery. Multi-stage strategies improve recovery by using graph-modified embeddings to guide the search while retaining the originals for semantic relevance. On questions requiring linked facts or comparisons among candidates, the strongest configuration improves recovery by up to 25.9% relative to plain vector retrieval. It also matches baseline recovery with 40% fewer returned nodes. Controls confirm that graph information contributes beyond multi-stage retrieval alone. Across the tested methods, retrieval performance depends more on how graph-modified embeddings are used than on how they are produced.

Abstract: Long legal documents are often represented as single retrieval units, which can dilute relevant content and limit retrieval effectiveness. This study investigates structural segmentation as a document representation strategy to improve Brazilian legal document retrieval. Using the hierarchical structure of legal acts, we represent each document as a collection of chunks. We evaluate four representation strategies: a multi-layered hierarchy representation and three simplified versions based on core chunks (articles and non-normative document components), with varying contextual details. We test these strategies on two publicly available Brazilian legal datasets containing normative acts and legislative proposals, using both lexical and dense retrieval models. Results show that structural segmentation improves dense retrieval compared with whole-document indexing, despite substantially increasing the number of indexed units. However, it does not consistently benefit lexical retrieval. Finally, we find that full hierarchicy representation is not necessary: core chunks enriched with document metadata yield comparable or better results.
